A uniform cut-elimination theorem for linear logics with fixed points and super exponentials
Esaïe B AUER & Alexis S AURIN

Abstract
In the realm of light logics deriving from linear logic, a number of variants of exponential rules have been investigated. The profusion of such proof systems induces the need for cut-elimination theorems for each logic, the proof of which may be redundant. A number of approaches in proof theory have been adopted to cope with this need. In the present paper, we consider this issue from the point of view of enhancing linear logic with least and greatest fixed-points and considering such a variety of exponential connectives. Our main contribution is to provide a uniform cut-elimination theorem for a parametrized system with fixed-points by combining two approaches: cut-elimination proofs by reduction (or translation) to another system and the identification of sufficient conditions for cut-elimination. More precisely, we examine a broad range of systems, taking inspiration from Nigam and Miller’s subexponentials and from Bauer and Laurent’s super exponentials. Our work is motivated on the one hand by Baillot’s work on light logics with recursive types and on the other hand by Bauer and Saurin’s recent work on the proof theory of the modal μ \mu -calculus.

1
Introduction

On the redundancy of cut-elimination proofs.

While cut-elimination is certainly a cornerstone of structural proof theory since Gentzen’s introduction of the sequent calculus, an annoying fact is that a slight change in a proof system induces the need to reprove globally the cut-elimination property. Such redundant new proofs are usually quite boring and fastidious, often lacking any new insight: cut-elimination results lack modularity. This results in the need of reestablishing a theorem which differ only very marginally from a previously proven one, even though the details are very technical and the failure of cut-elimination may hide in those small variants.
There are mainly two directions to try and make cut-elimination results more uniform, reduction and axiomatization:

Cut-elimination by reduction

The first option consists in proving a new cut-elimination result
by means of translation between proof systems, allowing to reduce the cut-elimination property of a given system to that of another one for which the property is already known. Very frequent in term-calculi such as the variants of the
\lambda
-calculus, this approach is also applied in proof theory, for instance in translations between classical, intuitionistic and linear logics
[
13
,
15
]
where linear translations come with simulation results. A more recent application of this approach is the second author’s proof of cut-elimination for
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
, the infinitary proof system for linear logic extended with least and greatest fixed-points, which is proved
[
22
]
by a reduction to the cut-elimination property of the exponential-free fragment of the logic
[
2
]
.

Axiomatizing systems eliminating cuts

The second option consists in abstracting properties ensuring that cut-elimination holds in a sequent calculus, and to provide sufficient conditions for cut-elimination to hold. For instance, after Miller and Nigam’s work on subexponentials
[
20
]
providing a family of logics extending
LL
with exponential admitting various structural rules, Bauer and Laurent provided a systematic and generic setting that captures most of the light logics to be found in the literature
[
16
,
18
]
,
superLL
, for which they provided a uniform proof of cut-elimination based on an axiomatization stating a set of sufficient conditions for cut-elimination to hold
[
6
]
. Another line of work, more algebraic, establishing sufficient conditions for cut-elimination is that of Terui
et al.
[
10
,
24
,
9
,
25
,
8
]
which established modular and systematic cut-elimination results by combining methods from proof theory and algebra.

We will see in the present paper that the two approaches can be mixed in order to provide a uniform cut-elimination proof for a large family of logics that we call
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
and that extends both
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
and super exponentials: we shall obtain a single proof for a large class of proof systems and, by relying on a proof translation-method, we shall not need to design a new termination measure but we will simply rely on simulation results from one logic to another.

Linear modal
\mu
-calculus.

One of our motivations originated in a recent work, where we established a cut-elimination theorem for the classical modal
\mu
-calculus with infinite proofs
[
5
]
.
A key step in this work consisted in proving cut-elimination of
\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
, a linear variant of the classical modal
\mu
-calculus,
to which we could reduce cut-elimination of the classical modal
\mu
-calculus.
Indeed linear logic offers powerful tools for translating systems like
\mu\text{LK}^{\infty}
from
[
22
]
and
\mu\text{LK}_{\Box}^{\infty}

[
17
]
into linear systems making the transfer of properties of those system to others logics efficient.
Proving cut-elimination for
\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
we were led to consider a more systematic treatment of exponentials and modalities revisiting a previous work by the first author with Laurent
[
6
]
and introducing
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
.

Light logics with least and greatest fixed points.

Taming the deductive power of linear logic’s exponential connectives allows one to get complexity bounds on the cut-elimination process
[
16
,
18
]
. Adding fixed points in such logic enriches the study of complexity classes
[
3
,
7
,
21
,
11
]
, as well as the study of light
\lambda
-calculus enriched with fixpoints as in
[
4
]
.

In
[
3
]
, enriching
elementary affine logic
with fixed points allows one to refine the complexity results from
ELL
, and to characterize a hierarchy of the elementary complexity classes. In
[
19
]
, it is even shown that the fixed-point-free version of this logic gets a very different characterization of complexity bounds for similar types.

The systems defined in the present article differ from those discussed in the previous paragraph: they are based on recursive types rather than extremal fixed-points (ie. inductive and coinductive types), we base our study on potentially infinite and regular derivation trees, etc.
However, both systems have strong similarities that we shall discuss in a later section,
which makes a stronger link between our systems and light systems from the literature.

Organization and contributions of the paper.

The main contribution of this paper is a syntactic cut-elimination result for a large class of (parametrized) linear systems wit least and greatest fixed-points coming with a notion of non-wellfounded and regular proofs.
In
\Cref
section:background, we recall some definitions and results about infinitary rewriting theory and linear logic; we also give definitions of a variant of Bauer and Laurent’s system of super exponentials
[
6
]
.
We set up in
\Cref
section:musuperll a parametrized system,
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
, which is
superLL
extended with fixed-points and non-wellfounded proofs.
Finally, in
\Cref
section:cut-elimination, we define the cut reduction system that achieve syntactic cut-elimination and provide the proof of our main theorem, the cut-elimination of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
, through an encoding into
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
.

2
Background on
LL
, fixed-points and non-wellfounded proofs

In this paper, we will study proof theory of different systems of linear logic (
LL
). It is much more convenient to work on one-sided sequents systems as proofs as well as the description of these systems are more compact than the two-sided version.
However, The results for the two-sided systems can be retrieved systematically from the one-sided systems with translations between them as in
[
22
]
for instance.

2.1
Formulas, sequent calculi and non-wellfounded proofs

Let
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{A}
be two disjoint sets of
fixed-point variables
and
atomic formulas
respectively.
The
(pre-)formulas
of linear logic with fixed-points are defined inductively as (
a\in\mathcal{A},X\in\mathcal{V}
):

F,G\hskip 5.0pt::=\hskip 5.0pta\lx@nobreakspace\mid a^{\perp}\mid X\mid\mu X.F\mid\nu X.F\lx@nobreakspace\mid F\invamp G\mid F\otimes G\mid\bot\mid 1\mid F\oplus G\mid F\with G\mid 0\mid\top\mid\wn F\mid\oc F.

Formulas
of
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
are such closed pre-formulas (
\mu
and
\nu
being binders for variables in
\mathcal{V}
).
By considering the
\mu,\nu,X
-free formulas of this system, we get
LL
, the usual formulas of linear logic
[
15
]
. By considering the
\oc,\wn
-free formulas of it, we get the formulas
\mu\text{MALL}^{\infty}

the multiplicative and additive linear logic with fixed points

[
2
]
. By considering the intersection of these two subset of formulas, we get the formulas of
MALL
the
multiplicative and additive linear logic
. The
\wn,\oc
-fragment is called
the exponential fragment
of linear logic.

Definition 1

(Negation)
.

We define
(-)^{\perp}
to be the involution on formulas satisfying:

\begin{array}[]{clclclcl}\bot^{\perp}&=1&X^{\perp}&=X&(A_{1}\otimes A_{2})^{\perp}&=A_{1}^{\perp}\invamp A_{2}^{\perp}&(A_{1}\with A_{2})^{\perp}&=A_{1}^{\perp}\oplus A_{2}^{\perp}\\[-2.0pt]
\top^{\perp}&=0&{a^{\perp}}^{\perp}&=a&(\mu X.F)^{\perp}&=\nu X.F^{\perp}&\lx@nobreakspace(\wn F)^{\perp}&=\oc F^{\perp}\end{array}

The sequent calculi that we consider in this paper are built on one-sided sequents: A
sequent
is a list of formulas
\Gamma
, that we usually write
\vdash\Gamma
.
Usually, in the literature, derivation rules are defined as a scheme of one
conclusion sequent
and a (possibly empty) list of
hypotheses sequents
.
In our system, the derivation rules come equipped with an
ancestor relation
linking each formula in the conclusion to zero, one or several formulas of the hypotheses.
When defining our rules, we provide this link by drawing the ancestor relation with colors. (See
\Cref
fig:MALLrules,fig:ExponentialRules,fig:fixFragment.)
As usual, some formulas may be distinguished as
principal formulas
: both formulas in the conclusion of an axiom rule are principal, no formula is principal in the conclusion of an
(\text{ex})
or
(\text{cut})
inference while in other rules of
\Cref
fig:MALLrules,fig:ExponentialRules,fig:fixFragment the leftmost occurrence of each conclusion sequent is principal.

Definition 2

(
MALL
,
LL
and
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
inference rules)
.

\Cref

fig:MALLrules defines
MALL
inference rules.

:
one-sided exponential fragment of
LL

Finally, inference rules for
\mu\text{MALL}^{\infty}
and
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
are obtained by adding rules of
\Cref
fig:fixFragment to
MALL
and
LL
inferences.

Figure 3
:
Rules for the fixed-point fragment

In the rest of the article, we will not write the exchange rules explicitly: one can assume that every rule is preceded and followed by a finite number of instances of
(\text{ex})
.
While proofs for
MALL
and
LL
are the usual trees inductively generated by the inference rules, defining non-wellfounded proofs for fixed-point logics requires some definitions:

Definition 3

(Pre-proofs)
.

Given a set of derivation rules, we define
pre-proofs
to be the trees co-inductively generated by rules of each of those systems.

Regular (or circular) pre-proofs
are those pre-proofs having a finite number of sub-proofs.

We represent regular proofs with back-edges as in the following example:

Example 1

(Regular proof)
.

We give an example of circular proof:

From that, we define the proofs as a subset of the pre-proofs:

Definition 4

(Validity and proofs)
.

Let
b=(s_{i})_{i\in\omega}
be a sequence of sequents defining an infinite branch in a pre-proof
\pi
.
A
thread
of
b
is a sequence
(F_{i}\in s_{i})_{i>n}
of formula occurrences such that for each
j
,
F_{j}
and
F_{j+1}
are satisfying the ancestor relation.
We say that a thread of
b
is
valid
if the minimal recurring formula
of this sequence, for sub-formula ordering, exists and is a
\nu
-formula and that the formulas of this threads are infinitely often principal. A branch
b
is
valid
if there exists a valid thread of
b
.
A pre-proof is
valid
and is a
proof
if each of its infinite branches is valid.

Example 2
.

A
, let us consider
{\wn}^{\bullet}A=\mu X.({A}\oplus(\bot\oplus(X\invamp X)))
and
{\oc}^{\bullet}A=\nu X.({A}\with(1\with(X\otimes X)))
.

\Gamma
and a valid proof
\pi

:

2.2
Cut-elimination for linear logic with fixed-point

Cut-elimination holds for
\mu\text{MALL}^{\infty}
and
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
in the form of the infinitary weak normalization of a multicut-reduction relation: a new rule, the
multicut (
mcut
)
, is introduced, that corresponds to an abstraction of several cuts.

†
†
margin:

Details in appendix
A.1
.

This rule has an arbitrary number of premises:

\vdash\Gamma_{1}

\dots

\vdash\Gamma_{n}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash\Gamma

and it is parameterized by two relations: (i) the
ancestor relation

\iota
which relates each formula of the conclusion to exactly one formula among the hypotheses and (ii)
the multicut relation
,
\perp\!\!\!\perp
, which links
cut-formulas
together.
\iota
and
\perp\!\!\!\perp
are subject to a number of conditions detailed in
\Cref
app:multicutdef.

Example 3
.

Representing
\iota
and
\perp\!\!\!\perp
in red and blue, the (
cut
/
mcut
) step is as follows:

To define the (
mcut
) reduction step we need a last definition, that will be also useful when defining the reduction step of the super exponential system:

Definition 5

(Restriction of a multicut context)
.
†
†
margin:

Details in appendix
A.2

Let

\mathcal{C}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

s

be a multicut occurrence with
\mathcal{C}=s_{1}\dots s_{n}
and
s_{i}
be
\vdash F_{1}\dots F_{k_{i}}
.
For
1\leq j\leq k_{i}
,
\mathcal{C}_{F_{j}}

is the restriction of
\mathcal{C}
to the sequents
hereditarily linked to
F_{j}
with the
\perp\!\!\!\perp
-relation.

The previous definition extends to contexts, writing
\mathcal{C}_{F_{1}\dots F_{n}}
.
For instance, writing
\mathcal{C}
for the premises of the rightmost mcut in
\Cref
ex:mcut,

\mathcal{C}_{B^{\perp}}=\{\vdash A,B;\vdash C^{\perp},D\}
while
\mathcal{C}_{A}=\emptyset
.

Cut-elimination for
\mu\text{MALL}^{\infty}
and
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
is proved syntactically with a rewriting system on proof with (
mcut
), whose steps are given in appendix
A.3
. As standard in sequent caluli, those (m)cut-reduction steps are divided in principal cases and (m)cut-commutation cases.

The cut elimination result is then stated as a strong normalization result for a class of infinitary reduction, initiated with proofs containing exactly one (
mcut
) at the root of the proof.
Indeed, strong normalization is trivially lost in such infinitary settings as one can always build infinite sequences that never activate some (
mcut
), thus converging to a non cut-free proof.

Fair reductions
precisely prevent this situation by asking that
no (
mcut
) that can be activated remains forever inactive along the reduction sequence.
The following definition is borrowed from
[
1
,
2
]
, residuals corresponding to the usual notion of TRS
[
23
]
:

Definition 6
.

A reduction sequence
(\pi_{i})_{i\in\omega}
is
fair
, if for each
\pi_{i}
such that there is a reduction
\mathcal{R}
to a proof
\pi^{\prime}
, there exist a
j>i
such that
\pi_{j}
does not contain any residual of
\mathcal{R}
.

This fairness condition allowed Baelde
et al.

[
1
,
2
]
to obtain a (multi)cut-elimination result for
\mu\text{MALL}^{\infty}
which, combined with the following encoding of exponential formulas using notations from
\Cref
ex:prom-encoding,
{(\wn A)}^{\bullet}={\wn}^{\bullet}{A}^{\bullet}
and
{(\oc A)}^{\bullet}={\oc}^{\bullet}{A}^{\bullet}
(extended to proof and cut-reduction steps),
induces the following
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
multicut-elimination result
[
22
]
:

Theorem 1
.

Every fair
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
(
mcut
)-reduction sequence converges to a cut-free proof.

3
Super exponentials

In this section, we define a family of parameterized logical systems, adapting the methodology of
[
6
]
and using the sequent formalism from the previous section. Consequently, the section lies in between background on the work by the first author and Laurent and new material since we propose an alternative system, with an alternative choice of formalization. We discuss briefly some of these differences here and shall come back to this comparison in the discussion of related works.
Bauer and Laurent’s super exponentials
[
6
]
only include
functorial promotion
and rely on the so-called
digging
rule to recover the usual
Girard’s promotion
rule.
On the other hand, we propose below another formalization of super exponentials, adapting the system to capture both functorial and Girard’s promotions primitively while we discard the digging which is not needed nor well-suited for the extension we aim with fixed-points.

This means that the general philosophy of this section follows that of
[
6
]
and in particular we show how their proofs can be adapted to the present setting in
B.2
. On the other hand, we will show in
\Cref
section:cut-elimination that our uniform cut-elimination theorem provides an alternative, completely new, proof of cut-elimination for the super exponential of the present section in the sense that it does not rely on adapting the techniques and proof by the first author and Laurent.
The first parameters of these systems will allow us to define formulas:

Definition 7

(Superexponential formulas)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a set.
Formulas of
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}{}(\mathcal{E})
are the formulas of
MALL
together with exponential connectives subscripted by an element
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}
:

F,G\hskip 5.0pt::=\hskip 5.0pta\in\mathcal{A}\lx@nobreakspace\mid a^{\perp}\mid F\invamp G\mid F\otimes G\mid\bot\mid 1\mid F\oplus G\mid F\with G\mid 0\mid\top\mid\wn_{\sigma}F\mid\oc_{\sigma}F.

Elements of
\mathcal{E}
are called
exponential signatures
. The orthogonal
(-)^{\perp}
is defined as the involution satisfying extending that of
\Cref
negationDef with:
(\oc_{\sigma}A)^{\perp}=\wn_{\sigma}A^{\perp}
for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}
.

Notation 1

(List of exponential signatures)
.

Let
\Delta=A_{1}\dots A_{n}
be a list of
n
formulas and
\vec{\sigma}=\sigma_{1}\dots\sigma_{n}
a list of
n
exponential signatures.
The list of formulas
\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A_{1}\dots\wn_{\sigma_{n}}A_{n}
is written
\wn_{\vec{\sigma}}\Delta
.
Moreover, given a binary relation
R
on exponential signatures and two lists of exponential signatures
\vec{\sigma}=\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{m}
and
\vec{\sigma^{\prime}}=\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
, we write
\vec{\sigma}\mathop{R}\vec{\sigma^{\prime}}
for
\bigwedge\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m\\
1\leq j\leq n\end{subarray}}\sigma_{i}\mathop{R}\sigma^{\prime}_{j}
.

While each element of
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}
induces two exponential modalities,
\wn_{\sigma},\oc_{\sigma}
, the inference rules will be described in two phases: first each
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}
will be equipped with a set of rule names
\{\wn_{\text{m}_{i}}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}\mid i\geq 2\}
which can be used to introduce the connective
\wn_{\sigma}
.
Second, some binary relations over
\mathcal{E}
will govern the available promotion rules, introducing
\oc_{\sigma}
.

Definition 8
.

The set of
exponential rule names
is
{\mathcal{N}}=\{\wn_{\text{m}_{i}}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}\mid i\geq 2\}
.

To each
exponential signature

\sigma\in\mathcal{E}
, one associates a subset of
\mathcal{N}
,
[\sigma]
.

For the sake of clarity, given
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}
we will write (when unambiguous)
\sigma
instead of
[\sigma]
, omitting
[\cdot]
throughout the paper.
We shall also switch freely from viewing
\sigma
(more precisely,
[\sigma]
) as a subset of
\mathcal{N}
or as its boolean characteristic function, write, for instance,
\wn_{\text{m}_{i}}\in\sigma
(resp.
\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}\in\sigma
) when convenient, or considering
\sigma(\wn_{\text{m}_{i}})
(resp.
\sigma(\wn_{\text{c}_{i}})
) as a truth value.

Definition 9
.

For one set of signatures
\mathcal{E}
, we define many systems, parameterized by three binary relations on
\mathcal{E}
:
\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}}
and
\leq_{\text{u}}
.
Rules for this system are the rules of
MALL
from
\Cref
fig:MALLrules in combination with the super exponential rules of
\Cref
fig:musuperllexprules: multiplexing (
\wn_{\text{m}_{i}}
), contraction (
\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}
) as well as functorial (
{\oc_{\text{f}}}
), Girard (
{\oc_{\text{g}}}
) and unary (
{\oc_{\text{u}}}
) promotions.

Each exponential rule comes with a side-condition written to the right of the premises.

Remark 1
.

Below, the side-condition for an exponential rule may also be written next to the rule label or simply omitted when it has been checked elsewhere. Those side-conditions are not part of the proof-object itself: all exponential inferences are unary rules.

Note that nullary multiplexing rule corresponds to usual weakening
(\wn_{\text{w}})
and unary multiplexing corresponds to dereliction
(\wn_{\text{d}})
.

\vdash\overbrace{\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate;

\vdash\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate;

\vdash\overbrace{\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate;

\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}

{\oc_{\text{g}}}

\vdash\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \immediate;

Figure 4:
Exponential fragment of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}

Definition 10

(
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
)
.

\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
proofs are the trees inductively generated by those inferences, satisfying the above side-conditions.

There are instances of
superLL
where cut-elimination fails: some conditions are required, so that cut inferences can indeed be eliminated.

The following two definitions aim at formulating these conditions in a suitable way:

Definition 11

(Derivability closure)
.

Given a signature
\sigma
, we define the derivability closure
\bar{\sigma}
to be the signature inductively defined by:

\sigma(r)

\bar{\sigma}(r)

\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{c}_{i}})\quad\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{c}_{j}})

\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{c}_{i+j-1}})

\sigma(\wn_{\text{c}_{2}})\quad\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{m}_{i}})\quad\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{m}_{j}})\quad i,j\neq 0

\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{m}_{i+j}})

\sigma(\wn_{\text{m}_{1}})\quad\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{c}_{i}})

\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{m}_{i}})

Derivability closure comes with the following property, proved by induction on
\bar{\sigma}(r)
:

Proposition 1
.

If
\bar{\sigma}(r)
holds, then
(r)
is derivable for
connective
\wn_{\sigma}
, using only inference rules
\wn_{\text{m}_{i}}
and
\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}
on this connective.

Notation 2
.

We name
\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}^{\bar{\sigma}}
(resp.
\wn_{\text{m}_{i}}^{\bar{\sigma}}
), for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, any derivation using only
\wn_{\text{c}_{j}}
and
\wn_{\text{m}_{j}}
rules and having the same conclusion and hypothesis as
\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}
(resp.
\wn_{\text{m}_{i}}
).
We write
\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{c}_{0}})
for
\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{m}_{0}})
and set
\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{c}_{1}})
to
true
for all
\sigma
and
\wn_{\text{c}_{1}}^{\bar{\sigma}}
to be the empty derivation.

To define a cut-reduction system, we consider cut-elimination axioms defined in
\Cref
cutElimAxs.
In
superLL
-systems each axiom corresponds to one step of cut-elimination.
However, as our reduction system with fixed-points is based on the (
mcut
)-rule,
some axioms will be used in several reduction cases.

\begin{array}[]{|clcll|c|}\hline\cr\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma(\wn_{\text{m}_{i}})&\Rightarrow&\bar{\sigma^{\prime}}(\wn_{\text{c}_{i}})\hfill i\geq 0&(\mathsf{Ax^{g}_{m})}\\
\sigma\leq_{s}\sigma^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma(\wn_{\text{m}_{i}})&\Rightarrow&\bar{\sigma^{\prime}}(\wn_{\text{m}_{i}})\hfill i\geq 0\text{ and }s\neq g&(\mathsf{Ax^{fu}_{m})}\\
\sigma\leq_{s}\sigma^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma(\wn_{\text{c}_{i}})&\Rightarrow&\bar{\sigma^{\prime}}(\wn_{\text{c}_{i}})\hfill i\geq 2&(\mathsf{Ax_{c})}\\
\sigma\leq_{s}\sigma^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma^{\prime}\leq_{s}\sigma^{\prime\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma\leq_{s}\sigma^{\prime\prime}&(\mathsf{Ax_{trans})}\\
\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma^{\prime}\leq_{s}\sigma^{\prime\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}&(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{gs})}\\
\sigma\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma^{\prime}\leq_{\text{u}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}&(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{fu})}\\
\sigma\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma^{\prime}\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}\land(\sigma\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}\Rightarrow(\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}\land\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}(\wn_{\text{m}_{1}})))&(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{fg})}\\
\sigma\leq_{\text{u}}\sigma^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma^{\prime}\leq_{s}\sigma^{\prime\prime}&\Rightarrow&\sigma\leq_{s}\sigma^{\prime\prime}&(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{us})}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

with
s\in\{g,f,u\}
, all the axioms are universally quantified.

For convenience, we use the notation
\wn_{\text{c}_{0}}:=\wn_{\text{m}_{0}}
and set
\bar{\sigma}(\wn_{\text{c}_{1}})=\text{true}
for all
\sigma
.

Table 1:
Cut-elimination axioms

In Bauer and Laurent’s system
[
6
]
, properties of
axiom expansion
and
cut-elimination
hold.
We defer the former to
\Cref
app:axexp and focus on the latter:

Theorem 2

(Cut Elimination)
.
†
†
margin:

See a direct proof in appendix
B.2

As soon as the 8 cut-elimination axioms of
\Cref
cutElimAxs are satisfied, cut elimination holds for
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
.

This theorem will be proved, in
\Cref
section:cut-elimination, as a corollary of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
cut-elimination theorem.
Many existing variants of
LL
are instances of
superLL
,
e.g.
let us consider
ELL

[
16
,
12
]
:

Example 4
.

Elementary Linear Logic
(
ELL
) is a variant of
LL
where (
\wn_{\text{d}}
) and
(\oc_{\text{p}})
are replaced by functorial promotion:

\vdash A,\Gamma

{\oc_{\text{f}}}

\vdash\oc A,\wn\Gamma

.
This system is captured as the instance of
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
system with
\mathcal{E}=\{\bullet\}
, defined by
\bullet(\wn_{\text{c}_{2}})=\bullet(\wn_{\text{m}_{0}})=\text{true}
(and
(\bullet)(r)=\text{false}
otherwise),
{\leq_{\text{g}}}={\leq_{\text{u}}}=\emptyset
and
\bullet\leq_{\text{f}}\bullet
.

†
†
margin:

Details in appendix
B.3

This
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}(\mathcal{E},{\leq_{\text{g}}},{\leq_{\text{f}}},{\leq_{\text{u}}})
instance is
ELL
and satisfies the axioms of cut-elimination.

As argued in
[
6
]
, the
superLL
-systems subsume many other existing variants of
LL
such as
SLL

[
18
]
,
LLL

[
16
]
,
seLL

[
20
]
.
The last two are particularly interesting as they require more than one exponential signature to be formalized.
In the following section, we will look at some examples for the fixed-point version of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
.

4
Super exponentials with fixed-points

In this section, we define
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
and give some interesting instances of it.

4.1
Definition of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an exponential name, the pre-formulas of
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E})
are
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}{}(\mathcal{E})
formulas extended with fixed-point variables and fixed-points constructs (with
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
X\in\mathcal{V}
,
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}
):

F,G\hskip 1.00006pt::=\hskip 1.00006pta\lx@nobreakspace\mid a^{\perp}\mid X\mid F\invamp G\mid F\otimes G\mid\bot\mid 1\mid F\oplus G\mid F\with G\mid 0\mid\top\mid\wn_{\sigma}F\mid\oc_{\sigma}F\mid\mu X.F\mid\nu X.F.

Formulas of
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}{}(\mathcal{E})
are the closed pre-formulas.
Negation is defined as the smallest involution on formulas satisfying the relations of
\Cref
negationDef as well as:
(\wn_{\sigma}F)^{\perp}:=\oc_{\sigma}F^{\perp}.

Again, for one set of signatures
\mathcal{E}
we define many systems, each parametrized with
\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}}
and
\leq_{\text{u}}
.
The inference rules for this system are the rules of
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
together with the fixed-point fragment of
\Cref
fig:fixFragment.
As before, pre-proofs of
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
are the trees coinductively generated by the rules of
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
and validity is defined in the same way as for
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
.

4.2
Some instances of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}

In this subsection, we give some interesting instances of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
.

4.2.1
A linear modal
\mu
-calculus

An application of super exponentials can be found in modelling the linear modal
\mu
-calculus introduced in
[
5
]
to prove a cut-elimination theorem for the modal
\mu
-calculus.
We show below how one can view a multi-modal
\mu
-calculus as
\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
as an instance of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
.

Let us consider a set of actions
\mathsf{Act}
.
Formulas of
\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
are those of
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
with the addition of a pair of modalities,
\lozenge_{\alpha}F
and
\Box_{\alpha}F
, for each
\alpha\in\mathsf{Act}
.
Rules of
\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
are the rules of
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
where the promotion is extended with
\lozenge
-contexts. Rules on modalities are a functorial promotion (called the modal rule) and a contraction and a weakening on
\lozenge
-formulas:

\vdash F,\wn\Gamma,\lozenge_{\alpha_{1}}G_{1},\dots,\lozenge_{\alpha_{n}}G_{n}

\oc_{\text{p}}^{\lozenge}

\vdash\oc F,\wn\Gamma,\lozenge_{\alpha_{1}}G_{1},\dots,\lozenge_{\alpha_{n}}G_{n}

\vdash F,\Gamma

\Box_{\text{p}}

\vdash\Box_{\alpha}F,\lozenge_{\alpha}\Gamma

\vdash\lozenge_{\alpha}F,\lozenge_{\alpha}F,\Gamma

\lozenge_{\text{c}}

\vdash\lozenge_{\alpha}F,\Gamma

\vdash\Gamma

\lozenge_{\text{w}}

\vdash\lozenge_{\alpha}F,\Gamma

(with
\alpha,\alpha_{1},\dots\alpha_{n}\in\mathsf{Act}
) The system considered in
[
5
]
corresponds to the case where
\mathsf{Act}
is a singleton, that is a calculus with two exponential names, one of these names representing the
\mu
-calculus modality rather than a linear exponential.

\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
can be modelled as the super exponential system
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
with:

•

\mathcal{E}:=\{\bullet\}\cup\mathsf{Act}
.

•

\wn_{\text{c}_{2}}(\bullet)=\wn_{\text{m}_{0}}(\bullet)=\wn_{\text{m}_{1}}(\bullet)=\text{true}
,
for any
\alpha\in\mathsf{Act},\wn_{\text{c}_{2}}(\alpha)=\wn_{\text{m}_{0}}(\alpha)=\text{true}
, and all the other elements have value
false
for both signatures.

•

\bullet\leq_{\text{g}}\bullet
;
\bullet\leq_{\text{g}}\alpha
;
\alpha\leq_{\text{f}}\alpha
for any
\alpha\in\mathsf{Act}
and all other couples for the three relations
\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}}
and
\leq_{\text{u}}
are false.

†
†
margin:

Details in
\Cref
app:mullmodIsMusuperLL

This system is
\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
when taking:
\wn_{\bullet}:=\wn,\quad\oc_{\bullet}:=\oc,\quad\wn_{\alpha}:=\lozenge_{\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad\oc_{\alpha}:=\Box_{\alpha}
.
Moreover, the system satisfies cut-elimination axioms of
\Cref
cutElimAxs.

4.2.2
ELL
with fixed points

In
[
3
]
, an affine version of second-order
ELL
with recursive types, called
\text{EAL}_{\mu}
, is introduced. This system allows only finite proofs.
Affine
means weakening applies to any formulas. Fixed points are added to a two-sided version with
\multimap
and
(-)^{\perp}
formulas, without any positivity condition on the fixed point variables, unlike what is enforced in our one-sided sequent version. The paper proves
\text{EAL}_{\mu}
cut-elimination and refines complexity bounds from
ELL
.

Considering
\mu\text{ELL}^{\infty}
, an instance of
\Cref
exa:ell with fixed points, gives us a typing system which is close to
\text{EAL}_{\mu}
. Namely, consider
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
with the same
\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}}
, and
\leq_{\text{u}}
as in
\Cref
exa:ell. Since the axioms in Table
1
only concern
\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}}
, and
\leq_{\text{u}}
, they are also satisfied by this instance of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
.

Our systems differs in two ways from that of Baillot: (i) the extremal fixed-points instead of generic fixed-points and the condition of positivity on fixed-point variables, and (ii) the infinite nature of our proofs. Thus, our cut-elimination theorem may not apply due to (i), and even if it did, it might not ensure finite proofs because of (ii). However, Baillot
[
3
]
uses only fixed-point variables in positive positions when proving complexity bounds, which addresses (i). Additionally, using only
\mu
-fixed-points to encode fixed points which ensures that cut-free proofs remain finite, resolving the incompatibility induced by (ii) by preventing infinite branches. (Moreover, the impact of weakening can be tamed by designing a translation making the system affine as well.)

Remark 2
.

Note that there is no proof of the conclusion sequent of
\Cref
exa:regProof in
\mu\text{ELL}^{\infty}
.

5
Cut-elimination

In this section, we only consider instances of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
satisfying the axioms of
\Cref
cutElimAxs. Let us assume given such an instance,
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
, that we simply refer to as

\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
in the following keeping the relations
\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}}
and
\leq_{\text{u}}
implicit.

5.1
(
mcut
)-elimination steps

Here, we define the (
mcut
)-elimination steps of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
.
To do so, it is suitable to have a specific notation for the premisses containing only proofs concluded by a promotion. We use similar notations to those of
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
cut-elimination proof
[
22
]
:

Notation 3

(
(\oc)
-contexts)
.

\mathcal{C}^{\oc}
denotes a list of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proofs which are all concluded by some promotion rule (
{\oc_{\text{g}}},{\oc_{\text{f}}}
or
{\oc_{\text{u}}}
).
Given
s\in\{g,f,u\}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\oc_{s}}
denotes a list of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proofs which are all concluded by an
(\oc_{s})
-rule.
In both cases,
\mathcal{C}
denotes the list of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proofs formed by gathering the immediate subproofs of the last promotion (being either
\mathcal{C}^{\oc}
, or
\mathcal{C}^{\oc_{s}}
).

We now give a series of lemmas that will be used to justify the (
mcut
)-reduction steps defined in

\Cref
def:musuperLLcutred.
We only give a proof sketch of
\Cref
ocfgreadinesscondition, and give complete proofs of each lemma in
\Cref
app:mcutstepJustif.
We start by the commutation cases of the different promotions.
The case
({\text{comm}}_{\oc_{\text{g}}})
covers all the case where
({\oc_{\text{g}}})
commutes under the cut:

Lemma 1

(Step
({\text{comm}}_{\oc_{\text{g}}})
)
.

If

†
†
margin:

Details in
\Cref
app:ocgreadinesscondition

\pi

\vdash A,\wn_{\vec{\tau}}\Delta

{\oc_{\text{g}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A,\wn_{\vec{\tau}}\Delta

\mathcal{C}^{\oc}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A,\wn_{\vec{\rho}}\Gamma

is a
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proof then

\pi

\vdash A,\wn_{\vec{\tau}}\Delta

\mathcal{C}^{\oc}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash A,\wn_{\vec{\rho}}\Gamma

{\oc_{\text{g}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A,\wn_{\vec{\rho}}\Gamma

is also a
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proof.

The case
({\text{comm}}^{1}_{\oc_{\text{f}}})
covers the case of commutation of an
({\oc_{\text{f}}})
-promotion but where only
({\oc_{\text{g}}})
-rules with empty contexts appear in the hypotheses of the multi-cut.
Note that an
({\oc_{\text{g}}})
occurrence with empty context could be seen as an
({\oc_{\text{f}}})
occurrence (with empty context).

Lemma 2

(Step
({\text{comm}}^{1}_{\oc_{\text{f}}})
)
.

If each sequent in
\mathcal{C}^{\oc}
concluded by an
({\oc_{\text{g}}})
has an empty context and

margin:

Details in
\Cref
app:ocfreadinesscondition

\pi

\vdash A,\Delta

{\oc_{\text{f}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A,\wn_{\vec{\tau}}\Delta

\mathcal{C}^{\oc}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A,\wn_{\vec{\rho}}\Gamma

is a
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proof, then

\pi

\vdash A,\Delta

\mathcal{C}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash A,\Gamma

{\oc_{\text{f}}}

is a
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proof.

We then have the following case where we commute an
({\oc_{\text{f}}})
-rule, but where there is at least one
({\oc_{\text{g}}})

Lemma 3

(Step
({\text{comm}}^{2}_{\oc_{\text{f}}})
)
.

If some
({\oc_{\text{g}}})
-rule in
\mathcal{C}^{{\oc_{\text{g}}}}
has at least one formula in the context and

\pi

\vdash A,\Delta

{\oc_{\text{f}}}

\mathcal{C}^{{\oc_{\text{g}}}}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A,\wn_{\vec{\rho}}\Gamma

is a
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proof, then

\pi

\vdash A,\Delta

\wn_{\text{m}_{1}}

\mathcal{C}^{{\oc_{\text{g}}}}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

{\oc_{\text{g}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A,\wn_{\vec{\rho}}\Gamma

is also a
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proof.

Proof sketch.
†
†
margin:

Details in
\Cref
app:ocfgreadinesscondition.

First notice that, by hypothesis,
\sigma\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{\tau}
. The proof is done in two steps:

1.

From
\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A,\wn_{\vec{\tau}}\Delta
we follow mcut-connected sequents until reaching one
\vdash\oc_{\sigma^{\prime}}A^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\tau^{\prime}}}\Delta^{\prime}
conclusion of an
({\oc_{\text{g}}})
-rule with
\wn_{\vec{\tau^{\prime}}}\Delta^{\prime}
non empty, for each signature
\sigma^{\prime}
in these sequents, we prove that
\sigma\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma^{\prime}
using axiom
(\mathsf{Ax_{trans})}
or
(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{fu})}
. Then we use axiom
(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{fg})}
to prove that
\vec{\tau}(\wn_{\text{m}_{1}})
holds and
\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\tau}
. Since
\vec{\tau}(\wn_{\text{m}_{1}})
holds, application of
(\wn_{\text{m}_{1}})
is allowed.

2.

We run through all the sequents and using axiom
(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{gs})}
, we prove that
\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}
for each signature
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
we encounter.

We therefore have
\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\rho}
as signatures from
\vec{\rho}
are contained on hypotheses of the
mcut
: the application of
({\oc_{\text{g}}})
is therefore legal.
∎

We then cover the cases where we commute an
({\oc_{\text{u}}})
-rule with the multi-cut.
The first case is where there are only a list of
({\oc_{\text{u}}})
-rules in the hypotheses of the multi-cut:

(Step

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A,\wn_{\rho}B

is a
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proof, then

\pi

\vdash A,C

\mathcal{C}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

is so.

Before giving the principal case for the multiplexing, we need to define
{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}_{\text{mpx}}}_{S^{\oc}}(\mathcal{C}^{\oc})
contexts. The intuition is that when a multiplexing rule reduces (i) with a Girard’s promotion, they simply cancel each other while when it interacts (ii) with a
({\oc_{\text{f}}})
or
({\oc_{\text{u}}})
, not only those two rules cancel, but also the other promotions hereditarily
\perp\!\!\!\perp
-connected to the first
({\oc_{\text{f}}})
or
({\oc_{\text{u}}})
rule,
until some Girard’s promotion is reached
, in which case this propagation stops:

Definition 12

(
{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}_{\text{mpx}}}_{S^{\oc}}(\mathcal{C}^{\oc})
contexts)
.
†
†
margin:

A graphical representation of this definition is given in
\Cref
app:mpxPrincipLemma,
\Cref
app:mpxPrincipompx.

Let
\pi
be some
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-proof concluded in a
\text{mcut}(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)
inference,

\mathcal{C}^{\oc}
a context of the multicut which is a tree with respect to a cut-relation
\perp\!\!\!\perp
and

S^{\oc}
be a sequent of
\mathcal{C}^{\oc}
that we shall consider as the root of the tree.

We define a
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
-context
{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}_{\text{mpx}}}_{S^{\oc}}(\mathcal{C}^{\oc})
altogether with two sets of sequents,
\mathcal{S}^{\wn_{\text{m}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\oc},S^{\oc}}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\wn_{\text{c}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\oc},S^{\oc}}
, by induction on the tree ordering on
\mathcal{C}^{\oc}
:

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{n}
be the sons of
S^{\oc}
, such that
\mathcal{C}^{\oc}=(S^{\oc},(\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{n}))
, we have two cases:

•

S^{\oc}=S^{\oc_{\text{g}}}
, then we define
{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}_{\text{mpx}}}_{S^{\oc}}(\mathcal{C}^{\oc}):=(S,(\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{n}))
;
\mathcal{S}^{\wn_{\text{m}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\oc},S^{\oc}}:=\emptyset
;
\mathcal{S}^{\wn_{\text{c}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\oc},S^{\oc}}:=\mathcal{C}^{\oc}
.

•

S^{\oc}=S^{\oc_{\text{f}}}
or
S^{\oc}=S^{\oc_{\text{u}}}
, then let the root of
\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{i}
be
S_{i}^{\oc}
, we define
{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}_{\text{mpx}}}_{S^{\oc}}(\mathcal{C}^{\oc})
as

(S,{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}_{\text{mpx}}}_{S_{1}^{\oc}}(\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{1}),\dots,{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}_{\text{mpx}}}_{S_{n}^{\oc}}(\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{n}))
;
\mathcal{S}^{\wn_{\text{m}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\oc},S^{\oc}}:=\{S^{\oc}\}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{S}^{\wn_{\text{m}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{i},S_{i}^{\oc}}
;
\mathcal{S}^{\wn_{\text{c}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\oc},S^{\oc}}:=\bigcup\mathcal{S}^{\wn_{\text{c}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\oc}_{i},S_{i}^{\oc}}
.

We can now state the multiplexing principal case:

Definition 13
.

\Cref

fig:musuperllexpcommcutstep,fig:musuperllexpcommcutstep2,fig:musuperllexpprincipcutstep (with the applicability conditions stated in the corresponding lemmas) induce the (
mcut
)-reduction relation over
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{E}}
proofs.

Remark 3
.

No justification lemma is stated for
(\text{comm}_{\wn_{\text{m}}})
nor
(\text{comm}_{\wn_{\text{c}}})
as applicability of
(\wn_{\text{m}})
and
(\wn_{\text{c}})
only depends on the connective and not on the context.

Even though some reduction rules presented in
\Cref
fig:musuperllexpcommcutstep may seem to overlap, note that the applicability conditions of the Lemmas ensure that it is not the case.

\hskip-28.45274pt\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\text{Reduction}&\text{Name}&\text{Lemma}\\

Commutative cut-reduction steps of the
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
promotion rules

with
S
being the sequent cut-connected to
\wn_{\sigma}A,\Delta
on the formula
\wn_{\sigma}A
.

Figure 7:
Principal cut-reduction steps of the exponential fragment of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}

5.2
Translating
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
into
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}

We now give a translation of
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
into
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
using directly the results of
[
22
]
to deduce
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
cut-elimination in a more modular way:

Definition 14

(
(-)^{\circ}
-translation)
.

We define
(-)^{\circ}
by induction on formulas (
c
is any non-exponential connective):

c(F_{1},\dots,F_{n})^{\circ}:=c(F_{1}^{\circ},\dots,F_{n}^{\circ});\quad X^{\circ}:=X;\quad\forall\sigma,(\wn_{\sigma}A)^{\circ}:=\wn A^{\circ};\quad a^{\circ}:=a;\quad(\oc_{\sigma}A)^{\circ}:=\oc A^{\circ}.

We define translations for exponential rules of
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
in
\Cref
fig:superlltollRules. Other rules have their translations equal to themselves.
Proof translation
\pi^{\circ}
of
\pi
is the proof coinductively defined on
\pi
from rule translations.

{\small\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 18.50186pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\overbrace{A,\dots,A}^{i},\Gamma$}\hskip Figure 8:
Exponential rule translations from
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
into
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}

Since fixed-points are not affected by the translation, we have the following lemma:

Lemma 10

(
(-)^{\circ}
preserves validity)
.

\pi
is a valid proof if and only if
\pi^{\circ}
is a valid proof.

The goal of this section is to prove that each
fair reductions sequence converges to a cut-free proof.
We have to make sure
(\text{mcut})
-reduction sequences are robust under this translation. In our proof of the final theorem, we also need one-step reduction-rules to be simulated by a finite number of reduction steps in the translation, which is the objective of the following lemma. We only give a proof sketch here, full proof can be found in appendix
D.3
.

Lemma 11
.

Let
\pi_{0}
be a
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
proof and let
\pi_{0}\rightsquigarrow\pi_{1}
be a
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
step of reduction. There exist a finite number of
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
proofs
\theta_{0},\dots,\theta_{n}
such that
\theta_{0}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow\theta_{n},\quad\pi_{0}^{\circ}=\theta_{0}
and
\theta_{n}=\pi_{1}^{\circ}
up to a finite number of rule permutations, done only on rules that just permuted down the
(\text{mcut})
.

Proof sketch.
†
†
margin:

Details in
\Cref
app:detailsredSeqTranslationFiniteness.

Non exponential cases and commutations of multiplexing or contraction are immediate.
Promotion commutations translate to commutation
rules and promotion key-cases.
We must ensure that there exists a sequence of reductions commuting the translation of each promotion.
Key-cases are trickier as they do not send the rules in the correct order: we need rule permutations to recover the translation of the target proof of the step.
∎

Now that we know that a step of (
mcut
)-reduction in
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
translates to some steps of (
mcut
)-reduction
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
, the following lemma allows us to control the fairness:

Lemma 12

(Completeness of the (
mcut
)-reduction system)
.

If there is a
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
-redex
\mathcal{R}
sending
\pi^{\circ}
to
{\pi^{\prime}}^{\circ}
then there exists a
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
-redex
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
sending
\pi
to a proof
\pi^{\prime\prime}
, such that in the translation of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{R}
is applied.

†
†
margin:

See proof in appendix, lemma
29

We define rule permutation with precision in appendix
D.2
. Here we show that validity is preserved if each rule is permuted a finite number of times:

Proposition 2
.
†
†
margin:

See proof in Appendix,
\Cref
app:validFinitePerm

If
\pi
is a
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
pre-proof sent to a pre-proof
\pi^{\prime}
, via a permutation for which the permutation of one particular rule is finite, then
\pi
is valid if and only if
\pi^{\prime}
is.

Corollary 1
.

For every fair
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
reduction sequences
(\pi_{i})_{i\in\omega}
, there exists:

•

a fair
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
reduction sequence
(\theta_{i})_{i\in\omega}
;

•

a sequence of strictly increasing
(\varphi(i))_{i\in\omega}
natural numbers;

•

for each
i
, an integer
k_{i}
and a finite sequence of rule permutations
(p_{i}^{k})_{k\in\llbracket 0,k_{i}-1\rrbracket}
starting from
\pi_{i}^{\circ}
and ending
\theta_{\varphi(i)}
. For convenience in the proof, let’s denote by
(\pi_{i}^{k})_{k\in\llbracket 0,k_{i}\rrbracket}
be the sequence of proofs associated to the permutation;

•

for all
i>i^{\prime}
,
p_{i}^{k}>p_{i}^{k^{\prime}}
if
k^{\prime}\in\llbracket 0,k_{i^{\prime}}-1
and
k\geq k_{i^{\prime}}
;

•

for all
i,k
,
p_{i}^{k}
are positions lower than the multicuts in
\pi_{i}^{\circ}
.

•

for each
i^{\prime}\geq i
and for each
k\in\llbracket 0,k_{i}-1\rrbracket,p^{k}_{i^{\prime}}=p^{k}_{i}

†
†
margin:

See details on proof in
\Cref
app:FairredSeqTranslationFiniteness

Proof sketch.

We construct the sequence by induction on the steps of reductions of
(\pi_{i})_{i\in\omega}
, starting with
\theta_{0}=\pi_{0}^{\circ}
,
\varphi(0)=0
and
k_{0}=0
and then applying
\Cref
redSeqTranslationFiniteness for each of the following steps.
We get fairness of
(\theta_{i})_{i\in\omega}
from
\Cref
mcutonestepCompleteness.
∎

Finally, we have our main result, proving cut-elimination of
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
:

Theorem 3
.

If the axioms of
\Cref
cutElimAxs are satisfied, then every fair (
mcut
)-reduction sequence of
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
converges to a
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
cut-free proof.

Proof sketch, see full proof in appendix,
\Cref
app:musuperLLmodinfCutElim.

Consider
(\pi_{i})_{i\in 1+\lambda}
,
\lambda\in\omega+1
,
a fair
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
cut-reduction sequence. If the sequence is finite, we use
\Cref
redSeqTranslationFiniteness and we are done.
If the sequence is infinite, using
\Cref
FairredSeqTranslationFiniteness we get a fair infinite
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
reduction sequence
(\theta_{i})_{i\in\omega}
.
By
\Cref
thm:mullcutelim, we know that
(\theta_{i})_{i\in\omega}
converges to a cut-free proof
\theta
of
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
.
We prove that
(\pi_{i})_{i\in\omega}
converges to a
\textnormal{{$\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
pre-proof using the fact that
(\theta_{i})_{i}
is the translation of
(\pi_{i})_{i}
and that it is productive.

Validity of the limit
\pi
of
(\pi_{i})_{i}
follows from the translation of
\pi
being equal to
\theta
up to rule-permutation (each particular rule permutes finitely). From
\Cref
circValidityRobustness and
\Cref
prop:validFinitePerm, these two operations preserve validity, therefore
\pi
is valid which concludes the proof.
∎

An important remark is that the above proof does not rely on
\Cref
superllcutelim in any way. As a consequence, cut-elimination for
superLL
is in fact a direct corollary of
\Cref
musuperLLinfCutElim:

†
†
margin:

See proof in appendix,
\Cref
app:superllcutelim2

Corollary 2

(Cut Elimination for
superLL
, that is,
\Cref
superllcutelim)
.

Cut elimination holds for
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
as soon as the 8 cut-elimination axioms of definition
1
are satisfied.

Remark 4
.

This result not only gives another way of proving cut-elimination for
superLL
-systems but the reductions we build in it are generally different from the ones that are built in
[
6
]
. Indeed, we are eliminating cuts from the bottom of the proof using the multicut rule whereas in
[
6
]
the deepest cuts in the proof are eliminated first.

Since
\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
and
\mu\text{ELL}^{\infty}
are instances of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
satisfying the cut-elimination axioms, we have the following results as immediate corollaries of
\Cref
musuperLLinfCutElim:

Corollary 3

(Cut Elimination for
\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
)
.

Cut elimination holds for
\mu\text{LL}_{\Box}^{\infty}
.

Corollary 4

(Cut Elimination for
\mu\text{ELL}^{\infty}
)
.

Cut elimination holds for
\mu\text{ELL}^{\infty}
.

6
Conclusion

We introduced a family of logical systems,
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
, and proved a syntactic cut-elimination theorem for them. Our systems features various exponential modalities with least and greatest fixed-points in the setting of circular and non-wellfounded proofs. Our aim in doing so is to develop a methodology to make cut-elimination proofs more uniform and reusable. A key feature of our development is to combine proof-theoretical methods for establishing cut-elimination properties using translation and simulation results with axiomatization of sufficient conditions for cut-elimination.

While our initial motivation was to make more systematic a key step in our recent proof of cut-elimination for the modal
\mu
-calculus
[
5
]
, this allowed us to generalize our previous result (capturing directly the multi-modal
\mu
-calculus without needing for a proof) but also to capture various extensions of light logics with induction and coinuctions, notably a calculus close to Baillot
\text{EAL}_{\mu}
.
Our system therefore encompasses various fixed-point extensions of existing linear logic systems, including well-known light logics extended with least and greatest fixed-points and a non-well-founded proof system. We provide a relatively simple and uniform proof of cut-elimination for these extensions.
Quite interestingly, the addition of fixed-points provide a new cut-elimination proof for the fixed-point free setting.

The
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
system, as defined in this paper, does not include the digging rule.
We plan to work on this question in future work, at least for restrictions of the digging.
Indeed digging is a very challenging rule wrt to its possible modelling using fixed-points as it would contradict the finiteness of the Fisher-Ladner closure, a basic property of fixed-point systems. On the other hand, incorporating digging would enable us to cover all of the super exponential version from
[
6
]
while our current system is incomparable with that of
[
6
]
. It could also be relevant for modal calculus, as the digging rule for modal formulas is equivalent to Axiom 4 of modal logic. Other modal logic axioms, such as Axiom T and co-dereliction rules from differential linear logic, can be viewed as rules in linear logic.

Another natural future work would be to explore linear translations of affine linear logic and/or intuitionistic/classical translations of these systems, facilitating the study of proof theory closer to
[
3
]
.

Finally, while we started with non-wellfounded proofs, studying how these results can be adapted to finitary version of
\mu\text{superLL}^{\infty}
is another interesting open question.

Appendix A
Details on
\Cref
section:background

A.1
Details on the multicut rule (
\Cref
multicutdef)

We recall the conditions on the multi-cut rule
[
2
,
14
,
22
]
.
The multi-cut rule is a rule with an arbitrary number of hypotheses:

\vdash\Delta_{1}

\dots

\vdash\Delta_{n}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

\vdash\Delta

Let
C:=\{(i,j)\mid i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,\#\Delta_{i}\rrbracket\}
,

\iota
is a map from
\llbracket 1,\#\Delta\rrbracket
to
C
and
\perp\!\!\!\perp
is binary a relation on
C
:

•

The map
\iota
is injective;

•

The relation
\perp\!\!\!\perp
is defined for
C\setminus\iota
, and is total for this set;

•

The relation
\perp\!\!\!\perp
is symmetric;

•

Each index can be related at most once to another one;

•

If
(i,j)\perp\!\!\!\perp(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
, then the
\Delta_{i}[j]=(\Delta_{i^{\prime}}[j^{\prime}])^{\perp}
;

•

The relation on premisses sequents defined as:
\{(i,i^{\prime})\mid\exists j,j^{\prime},(i,j)\perp\!\!\!\perp(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}
is acyclic and connected.

A.2
Details on the restriction of a multicut context (
\Cref
multicutRestriction)

Definition 15

(Restriction of a multicut context)
.

Let

\mathcal{C}

\mathsf{mcut(\iota,\perp\!\!\!\perp)}

s

be a multicut occurrence such that
\mathcal{C}=s_{1}\quad\dots\quad s_{n}
and let
s_{i}:=\vdash G_{1},\dots,G_{r_{i}}
, we define
\mathcal{C}_{G_{j}}
with
G_{j}\in s_{i}
to be the least sub-context of
\mathcal{C}
such that:

•

The sequent
s_{i}
is in
\mathcal{C}_{G_{j}}
;

•

If there exists
l
such that
(i,j)\perp\!\!\!\perp(k,l)
then
s_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{G_{j}}
;

•

For any
k\neq i
, if there exists
l
such that
(k,l)\perp\!\!\!\perp(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
and that
s_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{G_{j}}
then
s_{k^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{C}_{G_{j}}
.

We then extend the notation to contexts, setting
\mathcal{C}_{\emptyset}:=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{C}_{F,\Gamma}:=\mathcal{C}_{F}\cup\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
.

\mu\text{MALL}^{\infty}

\Cref
fig:muMALLonestepPrincip.

One-step multicut-elimination for
\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}

\mu\text{LL}^{\infty}
are steps from
\mu\text{MALL}^{\infty}
together with the reduction of the exponential fragment given in
\Cref

Multicut-elimination steps of the exponential fragment of

Appendix B
Details on
\Cref
secsuperLL

B.1
Proof of Axiom Expansion property

Lemma 13

(Axiom Expansion)
.

One-step axiom expansion holds for formulas
\wn_{\sigma}A
and
\oc_{\sigma}A
in
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
if
\sigma
satisfies the following
expansion axiom
:

\sigma\leq_{\text{u}}\sigma\quad\lor\quad\sigma\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma\quad\lor\quad(\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma\land\sigma(\wn_{\text{m}_{1}})).

The axiom expansion holds in
\textnormal{{$\text{superLL}$}}(\mathcal{E},\leq_{\text{g}},\leq_{\text{f}},\leq_{\text{u}})
if all
\sigma
satisfy the expansion axiom.

Proof.

We start by proving the first part of the theorem. We distinguish three cases depending on which branch of the disjunction holds for
\sigma
:

•

If
\sigma\leq_{\text{u}}\sigma
is true, then we have:

\vdash A^{\perp},A

\sigma\leq_{\text{u}}\sigma

{\oc_{\text{u}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A^{\perp},\wn_{\sigma}A

•

If
\sigma\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma
is true, it is similar to the previous case:

\vdash A^{\perp},A

\sigma\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma

{\oc_{\text{f}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A^{\perp},\wn_{\sigma}A

•

And if
\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma
and
\overline{(\sigma)}(\wn_{\text{m}_{1}})
:

\vdash A^{\perp},A

\overline{(\sigma)}(\wn_{\text{m}_{1}})

\overline{\wn_{\text{m}_{1}}}

\vdash A^{\perp},\wn_{\sigma}A

\sigma\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma

{\oc_{\text{g}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma}A^{\perp},\wn_{\sigma}A

The second part of the theorem is proved by induction on the size of the formula,
using the first part of the theorem.
∎

B.2
Proof of cut-elimination of
superLL
(
\Cref
superllcutelim)

We first need three lemmas called the substitution lemmas:

Lemma 14

(Girard Substitution Lemma)
.

Let
\sigma_{1}
be a signature and
\vec{\sigma_{2}}
a list of signatures such that
\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}
.
Let
A
be a formula, and let
\Delta
be a context, such that for all
\Gamma
, if
\vdash A,\Gamma
is provable without using any cut then
\vdash\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\Gamma
is provable without using any cut.
Then we have that for all
\Gamma
, if
\vdash\overbrace{\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A}^{n},\Gamma
is provable without using any cut then
\vdash\overbrace{\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta}^{n},\Gamma
.

Proof.

First we can notice that for any
\Gamma
the following rule:

\vdash A,\dotsc,A,\Gamma

S_{g}

\vdash\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\Gamma

is admissible in the system without cuts (by an easy induction on the number of
A
).

Now we show the lemma by induction on the proof of
\vdash\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\Gamma
. We distinguish cases according to the last rule:

•

If it is a rule on a formula of
\Gamma
which is not a promotion:

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 25.50186pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 36.96439pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\pi$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$$}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\Gamma^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to87.62904pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$r$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hskip 1.40277pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\Gamma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\rightsquigarrow\qquad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 27.09909pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 24.75749pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$IH(\pi)$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$$}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\Gamma^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to85.29857pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$r$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hskip 1.40277pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\Gamma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces

•

If it is a Girard’s style promotion, thanks to the axiom
(\mathsf{Ax_{trans})}
, we have:

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 22.75186pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 49.11021pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\pi$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$$}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{3}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-2.40091pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to218.49504pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.5398pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt${\oc_{\text{g}}}$}}\vskip-2.4009pt\hbox{\hskip 47.3799pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\rightsquigarrow

IH(\pi)

\vdash B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{3}}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma_{1}

\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

(\mathsf{Ax_{trans})}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

{\oc_{\text{g}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

•

If it is a unary promotion, we use axiom
(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{us})}
:

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 17.43242pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 11.6099pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\pi$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash B,A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{u}}\sigma_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to92.46883pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.22035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt${\oc_{\text{u}}}$}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 14.9876pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow

\pi

\vdash B,A

S_{g}

\vdash B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{u}}\sigma_{1}

\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{us})}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

{\oc_{\text{g}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

•

If it is a functorial promotion:

\hskip-14.22636pt\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 17.43242pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 17.52657pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\pi$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash B,\Gamma^{\prime},\overbrace{A,\dotsc,A}^{n}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{\sigma_{3}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to152.31387pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.22035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt${\oc_{\text{f}}}$}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 14.28932pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\quad\rightsquigarrow

IH(\pi)

\vdash B,\Gamma^{\prime},\overbrace{A,\dotsc,A}^{n}

S_{g}

\vdash B,\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma_{1}

\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

\hskip-14.22636pte_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{e_{3}}

(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{fg})}

\overline{(\vec{\sigma_{3}})}(\wn_{\text{m}_{1}})

\overline{\wn_{\text{m}_{1}}}

\vdash B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

\hskip-14.22636pt\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma_{1}

\hskip-14.22636pt\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

\hskip-14.22636pt\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{e_{3}}

(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{fg})}

\hskip-14.22636pt\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{3}}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma_{1}

\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{fg})}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

{\oc_{\text{g}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

) on a

:

Lemma 15

(Functorial Substitution Lemma)
.

Let
\sigma_{1}
be a signature and
\vec{\sigma_{2}}
a list of signatures such that
\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}
.
Let
A
be a formula, and let
\Delta
be a context, such that for all
\Gamma
, if
\vdash A,\Gamma
is provable without using any cut then
\vdash\Delta,\Gamma
is provable without using any cut.
Then we have that for all
\Gamma
, if
\vdash\overbrace{\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A}^{n},\Gamma
is provable without using any cut then
\vdash\overbrace{\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta}^{n},\Gamma
as well.

Proof.

First we can notice that for any
\Gamma
the following rule:

\vdash A,\dotsc,A,\Gamma

S_{f}

\vdash\Delta,\dotsc,\Delta,\Gamma

is admissible in the system without cuts (by an easy induction on the number of
A
).
Now we show the lemma by induction on the proof of
\vdash\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\Gamma
. We distinguish cases according to the last applied rule :

•

If it is a rule on a formula of
\Gamma
which is not a promotion:

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 19.50186pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 36.96439pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\pi$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\Gamma^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to87.62904pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$r$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hskip 1.40277pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\Gamma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\rightsquigarrow\qquad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 21.09909pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 24.75749pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$IH(\pi)$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\Gamma^{\prime}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\vskip 0.34909pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to85.29857pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.6509pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$r$}}\vskip 0.3491pt\hbox{\hskip 1.40277pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\Gamma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces

•

If it is a Girard’s style promotion. Thanks to the axiom
(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{gs})}
, we have:

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 16.75186pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 49.11021pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\pi$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{3}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-2.40091pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to218.49504pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.5398pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt${\oc_{\text{g}}}$}}\vskip-2.4009pt\hbox{\hskip 47.3799pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\rightsquigarrow

IH(\pi)

\vdash B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{3}}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\sigma_{1}

\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{gs})}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{g}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

{\oc_{\text{g}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

•

If it is a unary promotion, we use axiom
(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{us})}
:

\pi

\vdash B,A

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{u}}\sigma_{1}

{\oc_{\text{u}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A

\pi

\vdash B,A

S_{f}

\vdash B,\Delta

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{u}}\sigma_{1}

\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

(\mathsf{Ax_{\leq}^{us})}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

{\oc_{\text{f}}}

•

If it is a functorial promotion, thanks to the axiom

we have:

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 17.43242pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 29.471pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\pi$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash B,\Gamma^{\prime},A,\dotsc,A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{e_{3}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma_{1}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to176.20273pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 2.22035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt${\oc_{\text{f}}}$}}\vskip-1.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 26.23375pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\vec{e_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\rightsquigarrow

IH(\pi)

\vdash B,\Gamma^{\prime},A,\dotsc,A

S_{f}

\vdash B,\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{e_{3}}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\sigma_{1}

\sigma_{1}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

(\mathsf{Ax_{trans})}

\sigma_{0}\leq_{\text{f}}\vec{\sigma_{2}}

{\oc_{\text{f}}}

\vdash\oc_{\sigma_{0}}B,\wn_{\vec{e_{3}}}\Gamma^{\prime},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta

•

If it is a contraction (
\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}
) on
\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A
, we use axiom
(\mathsf{Ax^{fu}_{m})}
:

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 17.66505pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 17.2376pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\pi$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash,\overbrace{\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dotsc,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A}^{n+i-1},\Gamma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$(\sigma_{1})(\wn_{\text{c}_{i}})$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-2.22035pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to108.33072pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.72035pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$\wn_{\text{c}_{i}}$}}\vskip-2.22035pt\hbox{\hskip 10.9203pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\vdash\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\dots,\wn_{\sigma_{1}}A,\Gamma$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\rightsquigarrow

\vdash\overbrace{\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta}^{n+i-1},\Gamma

\vdash\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\overbrace{A,\dotsc,A}^{i},\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\Gamma

\vdash\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\Delta,\dotsc,\Delta,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\dotsc,\wn_{\vec{\sigma_{2}}}\Delta,\Gamma
